  That came out of a real cross-slice regression the T3 cold run caught:

  - Slice 5's history disclosure had no accessible name beyond its visible text,
  "History".
  - Slice 7 added an Edit button labelled Edit status for <job name>.
  - A fixture job named ...History Job therefore made getByRole("button", { 
  name: /History/ }) match two buttons — strict-mode violation.

  The two edits were:

  1. name: /History/ → name: /status history/i — following an app-side fix, not
  replacing one. Let me confirm that:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Confirmed — the one-line fix was in the app,
  frontend/src/components/JobRow.tsx:47:

  aria-label={expanded ? "Hide status history" : "Show status history"}

  So the two spec edits were:

  1. Selector follows the app fix — /History/ → /status history/i. The
  disclosure now carries a stable accessible name that never varies with job
  data; the test queries by it. The alternative would have been scoping the old
  selector more cleverly, which leaves the real defect in place: a button whose
  accessible name collides with user-supplied content.
  2. Fixture renames — History Job → Timeline Fixture, Toggle Job → Toggle 
  Fixture. Naming test data after UI copy is what created the collision in the
  first place.

  The distinction that matters for your question: no assertion was weakened and
  no test was deleted or skipped. Both tests still click the disclosure and
  assert the same timeline behavior — the change was to how they address the 
  button, driven by an accessibility fix to the component. Test counts went 77 →
  87 across slice 7, all additions.

  Worth noting this is exactly the class of bug the T3 cold-run discipline
  exists to catch: it was invisible in slice 5 and 6, and only appeared when
  slice 7's new button landed next to it.

  3. Database

  postgresql://jobs:jobs@127.0.0.1:55432/jobs

  make db-url prints this any time. For a GUI client (TablePlus, DBeaver,
  Postico):

  ┌─────────────────┬─────────────┐
  │      Field      │    Value    │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Host            │ 127.0.0.1   │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Port            │ 55432       │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Database        │ jobs        │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ User / Password │ jobs / jobs │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ SSL             │ off         │
  └─────────────────┴─────────────┘

  The port is 55432, not 5432, so it can't collide with a Postgres you might
  already have running locally. It's bound to 127.0.0.1 only — not reachable off
  your machine. Tables worth looking at are jobs_job (with the current_status /
  current_status_at projection columns) and jobs_jobstatus (the append-only
  event log). If you'd rather stay in the terminal, make psql drops you straight
  into a shell.

  One heads-up before you get attached to any data: make test seeds baseline
  fixtures and clears existing jobs first. Nina's job and anything else you
  create by hand will be wiped the next time the gate runs. Tell me before I run
  it if you want something preserved.

⏺ Read through all 15 files. I won't run a review of my own since you have one
  going — this is a map, not an audit.

  The shape: four layers, strictly one-directional

  api/client.ts      →  the only place that calls fetch()
  api/jobs.ts        →  endpoints as typed functions
  hooks/useJobs.ts   →  TanStack Query keys + queries
  hooks/useJobMutations.ts → writes, incl. optimistic update
  components/*       →  render only; never touch fetch

  Nothing below reaches up. A component can't make a network call without going
  through the layer above it, which is what keeps error handling in one place.

  All 15 files

  The four to actually read, in this order

  1. api/client.ts — the design claim is that every failure mode arrives at the
  UI as one ApiError: offline becomes status 0, a non-JSON proxy error page
  becomes a readable message, DRF's {detail, errors} becomes .fields so a
  message can sit next to the input that caused it. If that abstraction leaks,
  everything above it gets more complicated, so it's the highest-leverage file
  in the frontend.

  2. hooks/useJobMutations.ts:36 — useSetStatus, the most interesting logic you
  have. Note what the optimistic update deliberately doesn't do (line 61): it
  moves current_status but refuses to guess allowed_transitions or can_retry,
  because computing those client-side would be a second copy of the state
  machine. The rollback snapshots every cached page, not just the visible one.

  3. App.tsx — how it's wired together. Two things to look at: the filter is
  part of the query key (line 18), which is why filtering hits the server
  instead of narrowing loaded rows; and createBannerError (line 32) routes
  field-level rejections to the input and everything else to the page banner.

  4. components/JobRow.tsx — the state machine's UI consequences. Every status
  stays visible in the dropdown but only server-permitted ones are selectable
  (line 76), from job.allowed_transitions. Line 90 has my favorite constraint in
  the codebase: the stop-editing button can't be labelled "Cancel" because the
  dropdown next to it contains "Cancelled" — a harmless action must not look
  destructive.

  Two things your review may surface

  - jobsApi.rename and jobsApi.retrieve are unused — no component calls either.
  rename maps to PATCH {name}, which is covered by the slice-3 API specs, so
  it's tested but not reachable from the UI. Fine to keep as API surface, but
  it's exactly the kind of thing a reviewer flags.
  - JobFilters.search is defined and threaded through query() but no UI sets it
  — that's intentional, it's slice 10's job. Worth knowing so you don't treat a
  review comment about it as a real gap.

  Send me the review findings when it lands and I'll work through them before
  starting slice 8.

⏺ Three good items. Let me look at what's there before I estimate.

  Read 2 files, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Good list. Let me check two things that affect the estimates.
  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Investigated all three. Here's where they land.

  1. Timezone on timestamps — trivial, ~5 min

  lib/format.ts:3 builds one Intl.DateTimeFormat with no timeZoneName. Adding
  timeZoneName: "short" renders 25/07/2026, 13:50 PDT. I checked — no spec 
  asserts on rendered timestamp text, so nothing breaks. The storage story is
  already right (UTC in, viewer's locale out); only the display is ambiguous.

  2. Status filter — make it multi-select, ~30 min

  My recommendation is to keep and fix it, not remove it. Removing it throws
  away working, tested code, and docs/PLAN.md commits to a server-side status
  filter in slice 10 anyway (case F3) — you'd delete it now and rebuild it in
  two slices. What exists is already the hard part: views.py:56 filters
  server-side across the whole table off the indexed current_status projection.

  The work is genuinely small:

  - Backend — query_params.get("status") → getlist("status"),
  filter(current_status__in=[...]), validate each value. Empty list means no
  filter. The existing index still serves IN.
  - Frontend — Filter becomes a set, chips toggle instead of latch, "All"
  becomes nothing selected. The query key sorts the array so [RUNNING, FAILED]
  and [FAILED, RUNNING] share a cache entry.
  - Tests — update the existing filter test in 05-job-list-ui, add select-two /
  deselect-back-to-all cases, plus backend unit coverage for IN and validation.

  3. Counts — ~50–60 min, and your instinct fits the architecture

  You're right that a maintained counter is the correct call here, and it's
  cheaper than usual for a specific reason: services.record_status() is already 
  the single writer of the projection. The counter follows the projection, not
  the log — so it updates inside exactly the branch at services.py:49 that
  already decides whether the projection advances, in the same transaction,
  under the same row lock. Decrement old status, increment new. Create and
  delete are the other two touchpoints.

  Two things I'd flag:

  The reconciler isn't hypothetical — the seed command already needs it.
  seed_jobs.py deliberately uses bulk_create and bypasses record_status() (250k
  jobs can't be a quarter-million round trips). So counters will drift on every
  make seed. That's good news for the deliverable: a reconcile_counts command
  becomes real, exercised code rather than a README caveat, and slice 10 seeds
  250k rows so it gets demonstrated.

  The honest cost is lock contention, not drift. One row per status means every
  concurrent transition into RUNNING serializes on that row. Irrelevant at this
  scale, a real hot-row problem in production — the answer is sharded counters
  summed on read. That belongs in the writeup as a known limit, not built.

  Your orphaned-records note fits the same section: both are "the write path
  maintains a derived value; production needs a sweeper."

  The one thing I need from you

  Items 1 and 2 I'll just do — ~35 minutes, clearly worth it. Item 3 is the
  judgment call, because an hour is a 40% increase on the 2h33m currently
  logged, and that figure goes in the README.

  command, or have it call the record_statuses? Then the reconciler wouldn't be 
  so essential, it would just be a nice-to-have-at-scale for outages etc.       

⏺ You're right, and it collapses most of my drift argument. Let me check the
  seed command's structure.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ You're right, and it changes the design rather than just the estimate.

  seed_jobs.py:87 already computes final_status for every job. So the fix is a
  Counter tally per batch, applied inside the transaction.atomic() block that's
  already at line 101 — about four lines, no extra round trips. --clear (line
  64) zeroes the counters in the same place. Calling record_status() per job is
  the half of your suggestion I'd skip: that's the 250k round trips the command
  exists to avoid, and it can't backdate multi-event histories as cheaply.

  With seed handled, the remaining drift sources are only raw SQL and
  shell/admin queryset operations — i.e. someone deliberately going around the
  write path. So the reconciler drops to exactly what you said: nice-to-have for
  outages and manual intervention at scale.

  To be straight about the numbers though: dropping the reconciler saves ~10
  minutes, so ~45–50 rather than ~55–60. The bigger win isn't time, it's that
  counts become exact by construction on every path the app uses, instead of
  approximate-with-a-cleanup-job. That's a materially better thing to put in
  front of a reviewer.

  Recalled 1 memory, wrote 2 memories (ctrl+o to expand)

  was avoidable before offering it to you as a cost.

⏺ Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 47 lines
      238  **Described but not built** — honestly out of scope for a few-
           hour build: read replicas, a
      239  caching layer, `JobStatus` partitioned by time, approximate co
           unts from `pg_class.reltuples`.
      240  
      241 +### Counts by status — designed, deliberately not built       
      242 +                                                              
      243 +A dashboard wants a total and a per-status breakdown. The obvi
          +ous implementation is the one this                            
      244 +design specifically rules out: `SELECT current_status, COUNT(*
          +) … GROUP BY current_status` on                               
      245 +every page load is a scan of the whole table for a number that
          + changes by one at a time, on the                             
      246 +hot path, exactly like the `COUNT(*)` that cursor pagination e
          +xists to avoid.                                               
      247 +                                                              
      248 +The answer is to **maintain the counts on write instead of der
          +iving them on read**, and the                                 
      249 +architecture already has the one thing that makes it cheap: `s
          +ervices.record_status()` is the                               
      250 +sole writer of the projection, so the counter moves inside the
          + transaction that is already open                             
      251 +and already holds the job's row lock.                         
      252 +                                                              
      253 +| Path | Effect on the counters |                             
      254 +|---|---|                                                     
      255 +| `create_job()` | `PENDING` +1 |                             
      256 +| `record_status()`, when the projection advances | old −1, ne
          +w +1 |                                                        
      257 +| delete | current −1 |                                       
      258 +| `seed_jobs` | tallies each batch it builds and applies one u
          +pdate per batch |                                             
      259 +| `seed_jobs --clear` | resets to zero |                      
      260 +                                                              
      261 +Note the seed row. It bypasses `record_status()` by design — 2
          +50k jobs cannot be a quarter of a                             
      262 +million round trips — so the naive version of this feature dri
          +fts every time the database is                                
      263 +seeded. It tallies instead, which costs about four lines and k
          +eeps the counts exact on every path                           
      264 +the application actually uses.                                
      265 +                                                              
      266 +Two things would still be true, and both are the interesting p
          +art of the conversation rather than                           
      267 +the code:                                                     
      268 +                                                              
      269 +- **Drift is possible, just not from the app.** Raw SQL or a s
          +hell session that writes around the                           
      270 +  services can desynchronize the counters. Production wants a 
          +periodic reconciler recomputing from                          
      271 +  the projection and reporting the delta — cheap to write, and
          + its output is a genuine health                               
      272 +  signal, since a non-zero delta means something is writing ou
          +tside the service layer.                                      
      273 +- **One row per status is a hot row.** Every concurrent transi
          +tion into `RUNNING` serializes on                             
      274 +  the same counter row. Fine here, a real bottleneck at write 
          +volume; the standard fix is a                                 
      275 +  sharded counter — *N* rows per status, summed on read — trad
          +ing a slightly more expensive read                            
      276 +  for contention that scales.                                 
      277 +                                                              
      278 +The same reconciler answers a second question this design leav
          +es open: **orphaned `JobStatus`                               
      279 +rows.** The cascade is Django's ORM collector rather than an `
          +ON DELETE CASCADE` constraint, which                          
      280 +is sufficient while every write goes through the ORM but leave
          +s nothing at the database level to                            
      281 +prevent orphans if a delete fails partway. A sweeper reaping s
          +tatus rows whose job no longer                                
      282 +exists covers it, and is the same shape of job.               
      283 +                                                              
      284 +Left out because the assignment is a few hours and this is a s
          +ystems-design discussion, not a                               
      285 +requirement — but the design is settled rather than hand-waved
          +, which is why it is written down                             
      286 +here.                                                         
      287 +                                                              
      288  ---
      289  
      290  ## Testing

      214 +`status` repeats to select several at once, and the values are
          + OR-ed. Repetition rather than a                              
      215 +comma-separated list because it is what `URLSearchParams` and 
          +DRF's `getlist()` both produce by                             
      216 +default, so neither end needs a parsing rule. Omitting the par
          +ameter means unfiltered — there is no                         
      217 +"all" sentinel for the two sides to keep in agreement. An unre
          +cognized value is a 400 naming it,

          + `(current_status, created_at, id)`

          +osts about what selecting one does.

⏺ All three done, committed as c11d0c2, and the stack is back up at
  http://localhost:8080 for you to try.

  What changed

  Time zones — lib/format.ts now includes timeZoneName: "short", so rows read
  25/07/2026, 14:36 PDT. The zone shown is the viewer's; the API stays UTC end
  to end.

  Status filter — multi-select and deselectable. The old version latched:
  clicking Running was one-way and the only escape was the separate All chip.
  Now clicking an active chip releases it, turning the last one off returns you
  to everything, and All reads as pressed when nothing is selected — because
  that is the unfiltered state, not a sixth status. Server-side throughout:
  ?status=RUNNING&status=FAILED, OR-ed. I kept it rather than removing it since
  PLAN.md commits to a server-side filter in slice 10 anyway — deleting it now
  would mean rebuilding it in two slices.

  Two decisions worth knowing about:
  - An unknown status is a 400 naming the bad value, not an empty list — an
  empty list reads as "no jobs match" and sends someone hunting for missing data
  instead of a typo. That path had no test before; it does now.
  - Query keys sort the selection, so the same filter reached in a different
  click order is one cache entry rather than two identical fetches.

  Counts — README section written, nothing built. It covers the counter
  maintained inside record_status()'s existing transaction, the seed tally, and
  the two things that stay true regardless: drift is only possible from writes
  that bypass the service layer, and one row per status is a hot row whose
  answer is a sharded counter. The orphaned-JobStatus sweeper sits beside it as
  the same shape of job.

  Verification

  - 96 E2E pass, up from 87 — 6 new API filter cases, 3 new UI cases
  - 43 backend unit tests pass
  - T2 tier, not T3: nothing touched Docker, compose, the Makefile or
  dependencies
  - docs/SPEC.md and docs/TEST_PLAN.md updated with the repeatable-status
  contract and cases F3a/F3b/F3c

  I skipped backend unit tests for the filter deliberately — it's fully
  observable through the API, so the e2e specs cover it. Unit tests here are for
  what e2e can't distinguish, like case D3's orphan rows.

  Two things to flag

  The time-log entry is reconstructed, not measured. My timelog.sh start call
  was the one that hit the transient tool error, so no session was open. I
  appended 21:12–21:37Z (25 min) from file timestamps and marked it as
  reconstructed in the ledger. That's contrary to the "logged as work happens,
  never reconstructed" rule, so adjust it if my estimate is off — total is now
  2h 58m.

  Your background code review predates these edits. It was looking at App.tsx,
  api/jobs.ts, useJobs.ts and format.ts as they were before this commit, so
  findings on those four may be stale. Send them over and I'll reconcile against
  the new versions before starting slice 8.

⏺ 105 passed, one failed and one flaky. Let me see the actual failure.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The per-call callback approach was wrong — TanStack stores one mutateOptions
  per observer, so the second mutate() overwrites the first's callbacks. Same
  shared-observer root cause as the finding itself. Replacing it with genuinely
  independent per-call state.

  One correction worth flagging

  My first attempt at #5 was wrong. I used per-call mutate(vars, { onError })
  callbacks — but TanStack stores a single mutateOptions per observer, so the
  second call overwrites the first's callbacks. That's the same shared-observer
  defect the finding describes, just moved. The test caught it: the first row's
  error banner never appeared.

  The real fix drops useMutation for status changes entirely. useStatusChange()
  does the optimistic write, request, rollback and invalidation in a plain async
  closure, keyed by job id — two rows in flight are genuinely two pieces of
  state. Rollback is also now scoped to the single row rather than restoring a
  whole-cache snapshot, so one row's failure can't undo another's in-flight
  change.

  I also fixed a bad assertion of my own: I asserted a cancelled job shows "No
  further changes", but CANCELLED is terminal and retryable, so it correctly
  shows Re-run.

  Test status

  Last complete run: 107 specs, all passing — but two were flaky (passed only on
  retry), which is not good enough for a gate the graders run once. Both were
  assertions racing a timing window:

  - The skeleton test held the response open only 700ms → now 2500ms.
  - the status change lands in the job's history asserted server state right
  after an optimistic badge update → now waits on the PATCH response. That race
  was pre-existing, not introduced here.

  The run in flight is verifying those fixes. I'll confirm the count and flake
  status, then commit.

  substring «flow» instead of its unique prefix. make test seeds with --clear so
  only one such row normally exists — but that's exactly the isolation rule the
  project states (re-runnable without make clean).

  All 11 findings fixed

  Backend:
  - #2 views.py:56 — filtering now applies only when self.action == "list", so
  get_object() sees the unfiltered queryset. Two API specs cover it: a detail
  route under a mismatched ?status= returns 200, and /statuses/?status=bogus no
  longer 400s about a filter it doesn't have.

  Frontend:
  - #1 — the optimistic writer targets jobKeys.lists instead of jobKeys.all, so
  it never touches history cache entries. Regression spec opens a history panel
  first, then changes status, and asserts the PATCH is actually sent and
  survives a reload.
  - #3 — skeletons carry .skel-row; .rows .row can no longer match placeholder
  markup.
  - #4 — the row stays busy until the awaited refetch lands, so stale
  allowed_transitions are never actionable.
  - #5 — replaced with per-row state (details below).
  - #6 — a failed list renders no rows and no "No jobs yet"; the footer reads
  "Couldn't load jobs".
  - #7 — history is an infinite query with "Load older entries"; the caption
  says "newest N loaded" while more exists rather than claiming a total.
  - #8 — the field error belongs to the value that earned it, so editing clears
  it.
  - #9 — length counted in code points, matching Django and Postgres.
  - #11 — a 404 skips rollback, drops the history cache, and the banner reads
  "This job no longer exists — it was deleted."

  Docs:
  - #10 — coverage paragraph rewritten for slice 7.5, dangling † footnote merged
  into ‡.

  Two things I got wrong along the way

  My first fix for #5 didn't work. I used per-call mutate(vars, { onError })
  callbacks, but TanStack keeps a single mutateOptions per observer — the second
  call overwrites the first's. That's the same shared-observer defect the
  finding describes, relocated. The test caught it: the first row's banner never
  appeared. The working fix drops useMutation for status changes;
  useStatusChange() does the optimistic write, request, rollback and
  invalidation in a plain closure keyed by job id. Rollback is also scoped to
  the single row now, so one row's failure can't undo another's in-flight
  change.

  I wrote a wrong assertion — expected "No further changes" for a cancelled job,
  but CANCELLED is terminal and retryable, so it correctly shows Re-run.

  On flakiness

  The previous run was green but had one flaky test, B4 (over-long name). I
  could not reproduce it — 4 repeats, 48/48 passing. I'm flagging it rather than
  claiming it fixed.

  Chasing it did surface a real defect the review missed: B6 located its row by 
  the substring «flow» instead of its unique prefix, so it fails the moment the
  suite runs twice without make clean — the exact isolation property case A3
  promises. make test seeds with --clear, which is why it never showed. Fixed,
  and I checked the other specs for the same pattern — B6 was the only one.

  I'll confirm the final counts and commit once the run lands.

✻ Worked for 9m 34s

❯ OK great, why are these tests taking so long to run?                          

⏺ Fair question — it went 1.8m → 5.5m. Let me look rather than guess.

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 1 file, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
⏺ Found it. CI: ${CI:-1} in docker-compose.yml:68, which flips two config
  switches:

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Still in the build phase, so I'll give you the structural answer now and
  confirm with numbers when it lands.

  The main lever is one line

  docker-compose.yml:68 sets CI: ${CI:-1} — CI is always truthy inside the e2e
  container. That flips two switches in playwright.config.ts:

  retries: process.env.CI ? 1 : 0,
  workers:  process.env.CI ? 2 : undefined,

  So the suite runs 2 workers, on a Docker VM with 4 CPUs, for 107 tests. That's
  the dominant cost — roughly 54 tests deep per worker, serialized.

  What each test actually pays for

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      Cost       │                  Where it comes from                  │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ~1–3s floor per │ Every one does page.goto("/") — full page load, React │
  │  UI test        │  boot, list fetch against real Postgres               │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Video + trace   │ retain-on-failure still records every test and        │
  │ recording       │ discards the artifact on pass. Not free in a headless │
  │                 │  container                                            │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                 │ retries: 1 — a flaky test costs its failed run (up to │
  │ Retries         │  10s per failed expect, 30s test cap) plus a full     │
  │                 │ re-run                                                │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ My deliberate   │ 2500ms in the skeleton test, 800ms and 600ms in the   │
  │ delays          │ two new 07 tests ≈ 4s total                           │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  On the 1.8m → 5.5m jump

  Specs grew 87 → 107 (+23%), which doesn't explain 3×. The 5.5m run also
  carried a flaky retry, and that run was competing with the --repeat-each
  investigation I had going. So I don't yet trust the comparison — the clean run
  in flight is the honest number.

  What I'd change, and what I wouldn't

  Worth doing: video: "off". Traces alone are enough to diagnose a failure, and
  this is pure overhead removal with no behavioural risk.

  Worth measuring, not assuming: raising workers. 4 CPUs could support 4 workers
  and roughly halve wall time. But this cuts against the project's first
  non-negotiable — make test has to pass once, cold, on a grader's machine you
  know nothing about. More workers means more contention, and contention is what
  turns a 2500ms window into a flake. Going faster by making the gate more
  timing-sensitive is a bad trade here.

  Not worth it: sharing browser contexts between tests. It would help, but it
  couples tests to each other, which is exactly what the isolation rule (case
  A3) exists to prevent.

  My recommendation is video off, workers left at 2, and accept ~4–5 minutes as
  the price of a gate that doesn't flake on someone else's hardware. If you
  want, I can measure the video-off delta on a single spec once this run
  finishes — that's a 30-second experiment and turns the recommendation into a
  number.

  What's genuinely left is narrower than the plan implies — roughly 6–10 tests:

  1. route.abort() — network-level failure. Nothing covers this. It's the catch
  around fetch in client.ts:48 that maps a network failure to ApiError(status 
  0), which is what drives isRetryable and the Retry button. Entirely untested
  today.
  2. Malformed / non-JSON response. client.ts:62 has a branch for an unparseable
  body ("The server sent a response we couldn't read.") — a proxy error page or
  truncated response. Untested.
  3. 500 on DELETE — arrives with slice 8.
  4. Recovery after a failed mutation — E5 covers list recovery only.
  5. Slow responses as a UI state — partially there now, not systematic.

  Can it go at the end? Mostly yes — with one carve-out

  Defer the systematic sweep: yes. It's a verification slice; nothing depends on
  it. Running it after slice 10 is arguably better, because it then also covers
  search and pagination failures instead of needing a second pass. And if time
  runs short, a missing sweep is far less visible to a grader than a missing
  delete button or search box, given the per-slice error tests already
  demonstrate the capability.

  Don't defer items 1 and 2. Those two are untested branches in client.ts — the
  file every other network path depends on. They're about four tests, they're
  cheap, and leaving the error branches of your most-depended-on module
  unexercised until the final hours is how a real bug surfaces at the worst
  moment. I'd fold them into slice 8.

  The risk worth naming: slice 10 is the heaviest slice — 250k seed, latency
  measurement, possible virtualization — and the most likely to overrun.
  Whatever sits after it is what actually gets dropped. So deferring is fine as
  long as you're deliberately choosing the sweep as the thing you'd sacrifice.

  8, systematic sweep to run between 10 and 11?

❯ What is the systematic sweep? I wanted to skip any kind of sweeper and leave

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Two different things share the word "sweep" — that's my fault for using it in
  both senses. They're unrelated:

  The sweeper you decided to skip

  A production background job — periodically recomputes status counts from the
  projection and reaps orphaned JobStatus rows left by a failed delete. Runtime
  code, would run on a schedule in a real deployment.

  That decision stands. It's already written into the README as
  designed-not-built, and nothing I've said since changes it.

  The "sweep" in slice 9

  A test file — e2e/tests/09-fault-injection.spec.ts. "Sweep" here just means
  sweeping across the failure modes systematically instead of ad hoc: for each
  verb, make the server misbehave with page.route() and assert the UI does the
  right thing.

  // roughly what the file is
  await page.route("**/api/jobs/**", (route) => route.abort());       // network
  dies
  await page.route("**/api/jobs/**", (route) => route.fulfill({ status: 500 }));
  await page.route("**/api/jobs/**", (route) => route.fulfill({ body:
  "<html>502</html>" }));

  No production code, no background job, no runtime cost. It ships nothing to
  the app — it only adds specs.

  So "skip the sweeper, note it in the README" and "skip slice 9" are
  independent calls. The first is decided. The second is still open.

  On skipping slice 9 too

  Defensible, and cheaper than it looks, because four of its five named cases
  are already covered by specs in 05, 06 and 07. Skipping it wholesale costs you
  the systematic per-verb pass — a completeness argument in the writeup more
  than a correctness one.

  The part I'd still push back on is small: route.abort() and a malformed-body 
  response, about four tests. Those hit the two error branches in client.ts that
  nothing currently exercises — network failure mapped to ApiError(0), which is
  what drives the Retry button, and an unparseable body from a proxy error
  page. Every network call in the app goes through that file. Four tests to
  cover its two untested branches is a better ratio than most of what's left on
  the plan.

  My suggestion: fold those four into slice 8, drop 09-fault-injection as a
  slice, and let the README's testing section say the systematic per-verb sweep
  was scoped out with the per-slice error tests standing in. That keeps the
  honest-omissions framing you're already using for the counters.

  One housekeeping note either way: PLAN.md and TEST_PLAN.md both call slice 9
  the "fault-injection sweep" while the README now describes a production
  "sweeper". Same word, two meanings, in documents a grader reads together.
  Worth renaming the slice to "fault-injection pass" — I can do that whenever
  you like.

  Want me to make those changes now, or wait until the gate finishes and I
  commit the review fixes first?

⏺ Reordering done. The gate is still building — the base-image re-pull cost ~8
  minutes before a single test ran.

  Execution order is now 8 → 10 → 9 → 11

  Slice numbers stay put so commits, spec filenames and TEST_PLAN ids remain
  consistent with what already shipped; only the order changes.

  docs/PLAN.md — slice 9 moved after slice 10, renamed "fault-injection pass"
  (not "sweep"), with the reasoning recorded: it verifies rather than builds,
  running it after 10 covers search and pagination in the same pass, and putting
  it last makes it the deliberate casualty if time runs out.

  docs/TEST_PLAN.md — slice table reordered, and slice 8 now carries D5 plus two
  new cases.

  README.md — new subsection under Testing explaining that failure paths ship
  per slice rather than in one deferred pass, and stating plainly that the
  fault-injection pass is test code while the sweeper is a production reconciler
  that was designed and not built. Those two were one word apart in documents a
  grader reads together.

  One thing the reorder turned up

  Case E5's row claimed "Backend unreachable (route abort)", but its spec
  fulfills a 500 — an HTTP error the server did answer. So the matrix and the
  test disagreed, and route.abort() was never exercised anywhere. Fixed E5's
  description to match what it actually does, and added two cases for the
  genuinely uncovered branches:

  - E9 — route.abort() → the fetch rejection branch in client.ts that maps a
  network failure to ApiError(status 0), which is the value isRetryable reads to
  decide whether Retry even appears.
  - E10 — a non-JSON body. A 502 from nginx is HTML, and JSON.parse throwing
  there must not surface to the user as Unexpected token '<'.

  Both pulled forward into slice 8, per what I flagged earlier — every network
  call goes through client.ts, so its error branches shouldn't be the thing left
  untested until the final hours.

  Next

  Once the gate reports, I'll commit the review fixes and start slice 8: delete
  UI with an in-app confirm dialog (never window.confirm), row gone without a
  refresh and still gone after reload, plus E9/E10. I'll hold off starting until
  that commit is green, per the one-slice-at-a-time rule.

⏺ Still alive, not stuck — buildx bake has been running 16 minutes exporting the
  3.33 GB e2e image. Backend units already reported in the same run: 43 passed.

  Two things worth naming while we wait, both about that 16 minutes:

  The rebuild was avoidable. Only e2e/tests/* changed, and the Dockerfile
  already orders things correctly — playwright install (line 16) sits before
  COPY . . (line 18). What invalidated it was the node:22-bookworm base being
  re-pulled. That tag floats: it moves whenever upstream publishes. CLAUDE.md
  says every base image tag is pinned, and by version they are — but not by
  digest, so a grader's cold make build can pull a different image than the one
  you validated against. Pinning node:22-bookworm@sha256:… (and the same for
  postgres:16-alpine, nginx:alpine) would make T3 mean what it claims. That's a
  slice-11 decision, not something to change mid-flight.

  Chromium is re-downloaded on every base-image change — ~100 MB plus apt deps,
  about 8 of those 16 minutes. Unavoidable given the DockerHub-only constraint
  that rules out mcr.microsoft.com/playwright; worth a line in the README's
  testing section as a known cost of that tradeoff rather than something to fix.

  I'll commit the review fixes and start slice 8 as soon as the suite reports.

      159 +            results: page.results.filter((job) => job.id !== i
          +d),                                                           
      160 +          })),                                                
      161 +        },                                                    
      162 +  );                                                          
      163 +}                                                             
      164 +                                                              
      165  /**
      166 + * Delete a job, optimistically, with per-row state.          
      167 + *                                                            
      168 + * Optimistic for the same reason the status change is: the ro
          +w leaving                                                     
      169 + * immediately is the "UI updates dynamically" requirement, an
          +d the user has                                                
      170 + * already committed through a confirmation dialog.           
      171 + *                                                            
      172 + * Rollback restores a snapshot rather than re-inserting the r
          +ow, because a                                                 
      173 + * removal has no single field to put back — position within t
          +he page matters.                                              
      174 + * The cost is that a concurrent status change landing in the 
          +same instant would                                            
      175 + * be reverted with it; the awaited invalidation below correct
          +s that within one                                             
      176 + * refetch, and two mutations on different rows inside the sam
          +e tick is a much                                              
      177 + * narrower case than the one this avoids.                    
      178 + */                                                           
      179 +export function useDeleteJob() {                              
      180 +  const queryClient = useQueryClient();                       
      181 +  const [deletingIds, setDeletingIds] = useState<ReadonlySet<n
          +umber>>(() => new Set());                                     
      182 +  const [errors, setErrors] = useState<ReadonlyMap<number, unk
          +nown>>(() => new Map());                                      
      183 +                                                              
      184 +  const dismissError = useCallback((id: number) => {          
      185 +    setErrors((current) => {                                  
      186 +      const next = new Map(current);                          
      187 +      next.delete(id);                                        
      188 +      return next;                                            
      189 +    });                                                       
      190 +  }, []);                                                     
      191 +                                                              
      192 +  const deleteJob = useCallback(                              
      193 +    async (id: number) => {                                   
      194 +      setDeletingIds((current) => new Set(current).add(id));  
      195 +      dismissError(id);                                       
      196 +                                                              
      197 +      await queryClient.cancelQueries({ queryKey: jobKeys.list
          +s });                                                         
      198 +      const snapshot = queryClient.getQueriesData<JobPages>({ 
          +queryKey: jobKeys.lists });                                   
      199 +      removeCachedJob(queryClient, id);                       
      200 +                                                              
      201 +      try {                                                   
      202 +        await jobsApi.remove(id);                             
      203 +      } catch (failure) {                                     
      204 +        // A 404 is success. The user asked for this job to be
          + gone and it is                                               
      205 +        // gone — someone else got there first. Surfacing an e
          +rror for an                                                   
      206 +        // outcome that matches the intent teaches people to i
          +gnore errors.                                                 
      207 +        if (!(failure instanceof ApiError && failure.status ==
          += 404)) {                                                     
      208 +          snapshot.forEach(([key, data]) => {                 
      209 +            queryClient.setQueryData<JobPages>(key, data);    
      210 +          });                                                 
      211 +          setErrors((current) => new Map(current).set(id, fail
          +ure));                                                        
      212 +        }                                                     
      213 +      } finally {                                             
      214 +        // Its history describes a job that no longer exists e
          +ither way: on                                                 
      215 +        // success because it cascaded, on a 404 because it al
          +ready had.                                                    
      216 +        queryClient.removeQueries({ queryKey: jobKeys.history(
          +id) });                                                       
      217 +        await queryClient.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: jobKey
          +s.lists });                                                   
      218 +        setDeletingIds((current) => {                         
      219 +          const next = new Set(current);                      
      220 +          next.delete(id);                                    
      221 +          return next;                                        
      222 +        });                                                   
      223 +      }                                                       
      224 +    },                                                        
      225 +    [queryClient, dismissError],                              
      226 +  );                                                          
      227 +                                                              
      228 +  return { deleteJob, deletingIds, errors, dismissError };    
      229 +}                                                             
      230 +                                                              
      231 +/**                                                           
      232   * What to tell the user when a status change fails.
      233   *
      234   * DRF's 404 body is "No Job matches the given query." — the O
           RM's sentence,

⏺ Slice 8 is built and its own spec is green (9/9, first run). The T2 gate is
  running in the background.

  What slice 8 added

  ConfirmDeleteDialog.tsx — an in-app modal matching the design signed off at
  slice 1.5. All the CSS it needed (.scrim, .dialog, .job-ref, .row-actions)
  already existed from the mockup, so this was markup only. It names the job and
  states the consequence rather than asking "are you sure?" about something it
  hasn't identified. Focus lands on Cancel, not the destructive button — the
  dialog is reached by clicking Delete, so an Enter still held from that click
  must not carry through.

  useDeleteJob — per-row state, same shape as useStatusChange. Optimistic
  removal with snapshot restore on failure. Two deliberate choices:

  - A 404 is success. The user asked for the job to be gone and it is gone —
  someone else got there first. Showing an error for an outcome matching the
  intent is how people learn to ignore error banners. This is finding #11's
  second point, applied to delete.
  - Rollback restores a snapshot rather than re-inserting the row, because a
  removal has no single field to put back — position within the page matters.
  The tradeoff is that a status change landing in the same instant would be
  reverted with it; the awaited invalidation corrects that within one refetch.

  Delete sits outside the action chain in JobRow, so it survives isTerminal — a
  completed job is exactly the kind you eventually clear out.

  Specs — 9, all passing

  D1 (gone without refresh, still gone after reload, GET → 404), D5 (failed
  delete restores the row), D6 (an unrelated expanded timeline is undisturbed),
  cancel, Escape, and the already-deleted-elsewhere case.

  Plus an explicit "never opens a native dialog" test that registers a
  page.on("dialog") listener and asserts it never fires. Worth its own test:
  window.confirm blocks the event loop, so the suite would hang rather than fail
  — the difference between a red gate and one that eats its timeout telling you
  nothing.

  And the two client.ts branches pulled forward from slice 9:
  - E9 — route.abort(). Not an HTTP error; the request never completes, fetch
  rejects, and that maps to ApiError(status 0), which is what decides whether
  Retry appears at all.
  - E10 — a 502 with an HTML body, which is what nginx actually returns when the
  backend is down. Asserts the user sees "The server returned an error (502)"
  and explicitly not "Unexpected token '<'".

  One note: D5 needed no wording change after all. I'd expected to deviate
  toward a non-optimistic delete, but the optimistic-with-restore version
  matches the signed-off case as written, so the plan stands unedited.

  I'll report the gate result and commit when it lands.

❯ OK great, so T2 is running a subset of the tests right?

  ┌──────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │ Tier │                  Command                   │        Scope        │
  ├──────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │      │ make test-spec SPEC=08-delete-job-ui ·     │ one spec — seconds, │
  │ T1   │ make test-backend                          │  no rebuild, specs  │
  │      │                                            │ are mounted         │
  ├──────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │      │                                            │ rebuild + every     │
  │ T2   │ make test                                  │ spec — what's       │
  │      │                                            │ running now         │
  ├──────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │      │ make clean && make build && make test from │ same assertions as  │
  │ T3   │  pruned Docker, --platform linux/amd64     │ T2                  │
  │      │ check, then eyeball the DB                 │                     │
  └──────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  The 9/9 delete run I did earlier was T1 — that's the subset. It ran only
  08-delete-job-ui against the already-running stack, which is why it took 28
  seconds.

  What's running now is T2: a rebuild plus all ~116 specs across every file,
  which is why it's slow.

  Worth being precise about T3 too, since it's the one that sounds like it
  should be broader: docs/PLAN.md states it explicitly — T1→T2 is a scope axis, 
  T2→T3 is not. T3 runs identical assertions to T2. What changes is the starting
  state: pruned Docker, cold build, unmigrated database. It catches a different
  class of defect — files that exist only in a stale image layer, migrations
  assuming an already-migrated DB, dependencies resolving from a warm cache.
  Things that pass warm and fail cold.

  Slice 8 is a T2 slice, so this run is the right gate for it. T3 is due at
  slice 11, and at any slice touching Docker, compose, the Makefile or
  dependencies — none of which slice 8 does.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Seeding 250k in the background. While it runs — a decision I want to flag
  rather than bury:

  I'm planning to skip virtualization, and the reasoning should be in the README
  rather than silent. The server cost is what the measurement proves is flat;
  the client cost grows linearly with rows the user has loaded. But "Load more"
  is an explicit click, not infinite scroll, so reaching even 500 rendered rows
  takes 20 deliberate actions. Virtualization would add a dependency, complicate
  every spec that counts .rows .row, and buy nothing at the row counts this UI
  actually reaches.

  I'll state the threshold where I'd change that, and let the measured numbers
  decide rather than asserting it.

  Read 1 file, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Output's buffered through the pipe again, so nothing shows until it completes.
  Waiting on the notification rather than polling.

  Where slice 9 stands:

  Spec written and green — 9/9 (T1). Covers F1 (one page on first render,
  page_size=25), F2 (append without duplication, plus a full walk asserting no
  repeated or skipped ids), F5 (both the API-level 400 and the UI keeping loaded
  rows when the next page fails), F6 (job created mid-walk), F8 and F9.

  F8 is the one that earns cursor pagination its place: it deletes rows the walk
  has already returned, including the row the cursor's value came from, then
  keeps walking. Offset pagination shifts its window on every deletion and
  silently skips a row at each subsequent boundary; a keyset cursor holds a
  value, not a position, so nothing moves.

  One backend change. F5 expected 400 for a tampered cursor; DRF raises
  NotFound, so it was returning 404. I made the code match the spec rather than
  the other way round — a cursor is a query parameter, so a malformed one is a
  bad request, the same class of mistake as ?status=BANANA, and it's now
  reported the same way with the blame on the cursor field. A 404 there claims
  the collection doesn't exist and sends someone looking in the wrong place.
  BadCursorIsBadRequest is mixed into both paginators.

  Baseline measured at 100 rows: first page 10.6 ms, deep page 10.0 ms, filtered
  10.1 ms — via a script in the scratchpad that takes the median of 15 samples,
  so the README numbers are reproducible rather than hand-timed.

  Once 250k lands I'll re-measure the same three, and that comparison is F7.

          +r the same 25 rows 200,000 deep:                              
      213 +                                                              
      214 +| Query | Execution time |                                    
      215 +|---|---|                                                     
      216 +| Keyset seek — `WHERE created_at < … ORDER BY … LIMIT 25` | *
          +*0.101 ms** |                                                 
      217 +| `OFFSET 200000 LIMIT 25` — the same page, the other way | **
          +39.703 ms** |

          +s, and the count DRF would have run

          +. Neither number is a guess about                             
      222 +what happens at a million rows; both are measured at a quarter
          + of one, and the shapes are what

          +n table size.

          + roughly 8 ms, and walking 200,000                            
      233 +rows in costs about 9 ms more than the first page — the depth-
          +independence the design claims,                               
      234 +rather than a promise about it. The residual difference is buf
          +fer-cache behaviour on a larger                               
      235 +index, not the pagination strategy.                           
      236 +                                                              
      237 +**Virtualization: judged unnecessary, not overlooked.** Server
          + cost is flat, but DOM cost is linear                         
      238 +in what the user has *loaded* — and loading is an explicit "Lo
          +ad more" click of 25 rows, not                                
      239 +infinite scroll. Reaching even 500 rendered rows takes 20 deli
          +berate actions. Virtualizing would                            
      240 +add a dependency and complicate every spec that addresses rows
          + by selector, to fix a cost this UI                           
      241 +does not reach. If load-more became infinite scroll, or the pa
          +ge size grew, the threshold worth

⏺ Slice 9 is built; T2 running. The measurements came out better than I
  expected.

  F7 — the numbers

  Seeded 250,000 jobs (~1m50s), roughly 640,000 status rows behind them. EXPLAIN
  ANALYZE, same 25 rows, 200,000 deep:

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │                         Query                         │   Time    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Keyset seek                                           │ 0.101 ms  │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ OFFSET 200000 LIMIT 25                                │ 39.703 ms │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ COUNT(*) — what PageNumberPagination adds per request │ 30.557 ms │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────┘

  The seek is ~390× faster than the offset it replaces, and the count DRF would
  run on every page load costs more on its own than serving the page does.
  End-to-end over HTTP, medians of 15 samples: a 2,500× larger table costs about
  8 ms, and walking 200,000 rows in costs ~9 ms more than the first page.

  That turns the README's central claim from an assertion into a measurement —
  which matters, because "cursor pagination scales" is exactly the kind of thing
  a candidate says without evidence.

  Two scoping calls, both stated in the docs rather than silent

  F7 runs out-of-band, not in make test. Seeding 250k takes nearly two minutes,
  and the gate is the one command the graders run once. The spec proves the
  property the numbers are evidence for — the walk stays correct — and the
  numbers live in the README.

  Virtualization: judged unnecessary, not overlooked. Server cost is flat but
  DOM cost is linear in rows loaded, and loading is an explicit 25-row click
  rather than infinite scroll — 500 rendered rows takes 20 deliberate actions. I
  recorded the threshold I'd act on (~1,000 rendered rows, or if load-more
  became infinite scroll) so it reads as a decision with a trigger, not an
  omission.

  One backend change

  F5 expected 400 for a tampered cursor; DRF raises NotFound, so it returned
  404. I made the code match the spec: a cursor is a query parameter, so a
  malformed one is a bad request — the same class of mistake as ?status=BANANA,
  now reported the same way with the blame on the cursor field. A 404 there
  claims the collection doesn't exist.

  Spec is 9/9 at T1. I'll commit once T2 confirms the other 116 still pass —
  slice 9 touched shared pagination code, so that's the run that matters.

⏺ Slice 10's spec is written and green at T1 — 10/10, first run. T2 is running.

  What the pass covers

  I deliberately kept "500 on each verb" out of it. That already exists where
  each verb lives — GET in 05, POST in 06, PATCH in 07, DELETE in 08 — and
  repeating it would have inflated the spec count without adding coverage. All
  ten tests are ground nothing else touched:

  ┌────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        Area        │                       Tests                        │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ History endpoint   │ 500 and abort — its error branch had never         │
  │ failing            │ executed, and the failure stays contained to the   │
  │                    │ panel while the row keeps working                  │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Recovery after a   │ PATCH, POST and DELETE each fail once, then        │
  │ failed mutation    │ succeed on retry                                   │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Dropped connection │ Aborted PATCH rolls back and reports unreachable;  │
  │  mid-mutation      │ aborted create keeps what was typed                │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                    │ Create disables the form and says "Creating…";     │
  │ Slow responses     │ slow list keeps the toolbar and create field       │
  │                    │ usable                                             │
  └────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The design choice worth naming: every fault is injected once, not permanently.
  An app that reports a failure but can never move past it has handled the
  error only in the sense that it didn't crash. So each test asserts the second
  attempt succeeds and that the banner goes with it — a banner outliving the
  failure it describes is its own bug.

  One thing I fixed in my own work

  I titled a test "a slow delete keeps the dialog open and disabled" — but its
  assertions check the dialog closes, which is correct, because delete is
  optimistic and there's nothing left to confirm once the row is gone. The
  assertions were right and the name said the opposite. Renamed to "removes the
  row without waiting for the server". A misleading test name is worse than
  none: it's what someone trusts when the test starts failing later.

  Once T2 confirms all 135, I'll commit and that leaves only slice 11 —
  delivery, and the last review touchpoint.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ All 8 findings fixed. Note the gate run will drop your host ports — I'll bring
  them back with make up when it finishes.

  The two that mattered

  F8 was deleting jobs it didn't own. seen comes from the unfiltered head of the
  table, so seen.slice(0, 3) was "the three newest rows anywhere", not "the
  three newest of mine". With fullyParallel and 2 workers, another spec creating
  a job in the window between my seedJobs and the first page fetch would get
  hard-deleted — and my expect([204, 404]) guard swallowed it, so F8 stayed
  green while some other spec failed. That's a gate-breaker on the one command
  graders run once. Now it intersects with mine and asserts a hard 204.

  D5 wasn't testing its own name. useDeleteJob invalidates the list in finally,
  so the row came back from the server whether or not the rollback ran — the
  test would pass with the rollback deleted. I fixed it by stalling the
  reconciling refetch and asserting the row returns inside that window, then
  verified the fix by actually deleting the rollback and confirming D5 goes red.
  It does. That's the difference between believing a test works and knowing it.

  The rest

  - removeQueries(history) was in finally, so a failed delete evicted the
  history of a job that still existed and whose row had just been restored —
  contradicting its own comment. Moved to the success and 404 paths.
  - The dialog's busy state was unreachable. setPendingDelete(null) runs before
  deleteJob, so isDeleting was always false — the prop, the "Deleting…" label
  and the guards were all dead. Removed rather than resurrected: the dialog
  closing immediately is the intended behaviour, and my own spec asserts it.
  - Escape broke once focus left the buttons. The handler was on a non-focusable
  div, so clicking the explanatory paragraph sent focus to <body> and Escape
  silently stopped working. Now bound to document, with a focus trap so
  aria-modal="true" is honest rather than decorative, and focus restored to the
  trigger on close.
  - Two stale docs — README said 96 specs, TEST_PLAN's coverage paragraph still
  read "as of slice 7.5" and listed slices 9 and 10 as outstanding while its own
  table marked them done. That's the second time that paragraph has gone stale;
  it was finding #10 of the previous review too.

  I added three specs while fixing: the Escape-after-blur case the original test
  structurally couldn't catch (it presses Escape while Cancel still holds
  focus), and a Tab-containment test.

  Full gate running. Once it's green I'll report, restore your ports, and that
  leaves only slice 11 — where the AI-usage section still needs your draft.
✻ Churned for 12m 41s

⏺ Checked again — the only change in README.md is mine (the spec count, 96 →
  135). The AI section is still <!-- TODO(nina) -->, and nothing else has
  touched the file.

  Most likely explanation: it's sitting unsaved in an editor buffer. There's a
  VS Code process running against this repo. Worth a ⌘S and then git diff 
  README.md to confirm — if it shows up there, I'll pick it up from the file.

  On the re-run delay — I think you saw something real

  Don't write it off as transient yet. I found one confirmed gap and have a
  specific suspect.

  Confirmed: nothing threads TanStack's AbortSignal into fetch. client.ts never
  accepts one, so a cancelled query's request still runs to completion. That's
  real but probably not what you saw — it wastes a request rather than delaying
  the UI.

  The suspect is the ordering in useStatusChange:

  setSavingIds((current) => new Set(current).add(id));   // row shows "saving…"
  await queryClient.cancelQueries({ queryKey: jobKeys.lists });   // ← await
  writeCachedStatus(queryClient, id, status);            // badge finally moves

  The badge update sits behind an await. So the sequence you'd see is: button
  disables, "saving…" appears, badge still reads Failed, and only then flips to
  Pending. That matches your description closely — and re-run is where it'd be
  most visible, because it's a single click with your attention already on the
  badge, rather than a dropdown selection where you're looking at the menu.

  The fix is cheap: paint first, then cancel, then re-apply (the re-apply covers
  a refetch that resolved during the cancel).

  But I haven't reproduced it, so I'm not claiming that's the cause yet. Once
  the gate finishes I'll write a probe — re-run a FAILED job and assert the
  badge reads Pending within a few hundred milliseconds — and see whether it
  fails against the current build. If it does, that's the bug and the fix is
  confirmed rather than plausible. If it passes, I'll keep looking rather than
  change code on a hunch.

  Gate's still running; I'll restore your ports as soon as it's done so you can
  try to trigger it again yourself — a second observation would help,
  particularly whether the badge stayed stale for a beat or flickered back after
  changing.

❯ Playing around with it, some thing are definitely nice to have:

⏺ Good observations — all three are real gaps. Let me ground the estimates in
  what's actually there rather than guess.

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Here's the honest sizing. Numbers assume the same pace as slices 8–10 (9–16
  min each) and include specs, since nothing here ships untested.

  ┌────────────────┬─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │                │       Effort        │            Real risk            │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Search         │ ~1h30               │ Low — design is already written │
  │                │                     │  up                             │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ "Showing x of  │ ~1h                 │ Medium — breaks if search lands │
  │ y"             │                     │  too                            │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Column sorting │ ~1h safe / ~2h      │ High — collides with cursor     │
  │                │ complete            │ pagination                      │
  └────────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  Sorting is the one to be careful with

  It looks like the smallest feature and is actually the largest.
  Job.Meta.ordering is ("-created_at", "-id") with a matching composite index,
  and the cursor encodes a position in that ordering. So:

  - Every sortable column needs its own composite index ending in -id, or it's a
  full sort of the table at 250k rows — the exact thing slice 9 exists to prove
  doesn't happen.
  - Changing sort must reset the cursor. A cursor from one ordering is
  meaningless in another, so "sort by name" can't preserve your position.
  - Sorting by status is actively unsafe with DRF's paginator. I documented the
  limitation in pagination.py: the cursor predicate is built from ordering[0]
  alone, and rows sharing that value are paged by an integer offset. With
  created_at that's an edge case needing two inserts in the same microsecond.
  With current_status there are five distinct values across the whole table, so
  tie-offset paging becomes the only path — rows get skipped and repeated during
  an ordinary walk.

  Making status-sort correct means writing the composite-cursor subclass I
  described as "the fix, if it ever mattered". That's the ~2h number, and it's
  why I'd either scope sorting to name / created_at / updated_at, or not do it.

  Counts and search interact badly

  "Showing x of y" is cheap only while filters stay categorical. With maintained
  per-status counters, y is arithmetic — sum the five rows, or just the
  selected ones. No COUNT(*) anywhere.

  Add search and that collapses: no counter can tell you how many names match
  %combustor%. You'd need a real COUNT(*) over a trigram match on every
  keystroke — precisely the hot-path count cursor pagination exists to avoid.
  The escapes are a capped count ("showing 25 of 1000+") or an approximation,
  both of which are worse answers than the honest "25 loaded" the footer gives
  now.

  So they're not independent: doing both is meaningfully more than 2h30, and the
  result is a worse footer than either alone.

  What I'd actually do

  Search, and stop there — if anything. It's the most user-visible, the design
  is already written, and it doesn't compromise anything else. Sorting risks the
  correctness property that is the project's strongest technical claim; counts
  stop being cheap the moment search exists.

  Or nothing, and spend the time on slice 11. That's my actual recommendation.
  You're at 3h37m on a few-hours take-home; all three would roughly double it,
  and the README already presents search and counts as designed, deliberately 
  not built, with the reasoning — which for an EM take-home reads as judgement
  rather than absence.

  One gap worth closing cheaply either way: sorting isn't in that writeup, so a
  reviewer might read it as unconsidered rather than declined. Ten minutes to
  add it, including why status-sort is the interesting case. Want me to do that
  as part of slice 11?

⏺ Added both to the writeup:

  Sorting — declined, with the reasoning. The framing I used is that it looks
  like the smallest omission and is actually the largest: every sortable column
  needs its own composite index ending in -id, changing sort has to reset the
  cursor, and sorting by status would be incorrect rather than merely slow. With
  five distinct values in the whole table, every page boundary falls inside a
  tie group, so DRF's offset-within-ties path becomes the only path and rows get
  skipped during an ordinary walk. The closing point is the one worth making in
  an interview: shipping sort on four columns while silently corrupting the
  fifth would have been worse than shipping none.

  "Showing 25 of 1,204" — why it's absent. A total means COUNT(*) on the hot
  path, which is one of the two reasons cursor pagination was chosen. It's cheap
  with per-status counters while filtering stays categorical, and stops being
  cheap the instant search exists, because no counter answers "how many names
  match %combustor%". So the section closes by naming the interaction: the three
  omissions aren't independent — search and an exact total pull against each
  other, and picking either constrains the other. Left out together,
  deliberately.

  That gives you a coherent story for all three if it comes up: each was sized,
  the interactions were understood, and the decision was to stop.

  Still open, in the order I'd tackle them:

  1. Gate result — still running; I'll restore your host ports the moment it's
  done.
  2. The re-run delay — I still want to probe that with a timing assertion
  rather than leave it as "probably transient". It's the only unexplained
  behaviour outstanding.
  3. Your AI-section draft — still not on disk. Worth checking for an unsaved
  buffer.
  4. Slice 11 — the final cold gate, plus the base-image digest-pinning
  decision.

  The two observations I drew out, because the distribution says more than the
  total:

  The frontend cost more than twice the backend. The backend is a state machine
  with a projection — once record_status() existed, the rest followed. The UI is
  where every partial state lives, and that's where both review rounds
  concentrated.

  Review fixes are 17%, and that's the number I'd defend most readily. Round one
  found a bug that silently broke the critical flow for anyone who'd opened a
  history panel; round two found a spec deleting other specs' rows. Neither was
  reachable by the suite as written.

  I also stated plainly that two entries have reconstructed start times and are
  marked as such — a time log that hides its own gaps isn't evidence of
  anything.

  The assignment took a bit longer than I anticipated (see time breakdown       
  below), but a lot of it was waiting around for the agent. I initially went in 
  recording my interactions with Claude from the very beginning, but soon       
  realized that there would be some multi-tasking and waiting around / taking   
  pauses in between (plus I have an old-ish Mac and I didn't want to eat into   
  its 8GB RAM) so I stopped the recording. The transcript is saved in the docs  
  folder.                                                                       
                                                                                
  I spent a little time before working with Claude Code ramping up on the       
  different technologies and doing a little research by browsing the internet   
  and using Claude chat, which isn't captured here. When I was ready to start   
  coding, I went with an approach of iterating on a spec and test plan first,   
  coming up with a plan that involved smaller testable pieces, and then         
  executing on the plan. I wanted to be able to write the tests before the      
  entire app was finished but I didn't want to just write a bunch of failing    
  tests so I opted to split the work into slices that could still be tested     
  with an end-to-end playwright test. I did give instructions to generallu do   
  lighter-weight tests on an already-running build rather than the entire `make 
  test` every time, with `make test` at the bigger touchpoints.                 
                                                                                
  Even with the test plan and end-to-end testing along the way, I also wanted   
  to understand the code. Claude does produce a lot of code and I made sure to  
  review the pieces that did the core functionality - like creating a new job   
  and ensuring it appears in the list, updating the job status, deleting a job  
  and its job statuses. There were basically 3 categories of files - the files  
  with the core functionality that I read line by line and sought to understand 
  deeply, the files with some more boilerplate code/schemas that I sanity       
  checked and understood their general shape (docker files, makefile,           
  serializers), and the files that I essentially just made sure I knew their    
  purpose and that they exist (configs, CSS, etc.). I did ask the agent to      
  explain some parts of the code to me. I asked for some small changes, like    
  variable naming to make it more readable etc. Most of the bigger changes and  
  architecture decisions were made during design.

      309 +maintained in `record_status()`, the total for any status sele
          +ction is arithmetic over five rows —                          
      310 +no count, no scan. That design is written up under            
      311 +[counts by status](#counts-by-status--designed-deliberately-no
          +t-built).                                                     
      312 +                                                              
      313 +It stops being cheap the moment search exists. No counter can 
          +answer "how many names match                                  
      314 +`%combustor%`"; that needs a real `COUNT(*)` over a trigram ma
          +tch, on every keystroke, on the hot                           
      315 +path. The escapes are a capped count ("25 of 1000+") or an app
          +roximation from `pg_class.reltuples`,                         
      316 +and both are less honest than the number the footer shows now.
      317 +                                                              
      318 +So the three omissions are not independent: search and an exac
          +t total pull against each other, and                          
      319 +picking either constrains the other. Left out together, delibe
          +rately.                                                       
      320 +                                                              
      321  ### Counts by status — designed, deliberately not built

           ous implementation is the one this
